# Handoff brief: E/E architecture synthesis, version 2

Audience: a coding agent working in the repository `okumus96/Design-Space-Exploration` (Python, Gurobi).
Purpose: implement the next version of the design-space-exploration model and its evaluation, following the decisions below. Decisions are binding; implementation details marked "open" need a human answer before they are coded.

---

## 1. Context in five lines

- The repository implements a MILP that jointly decides software-to-site placement, ECU composition (partitions, hardware features, ports) and the in-vehicle network (wires, routing, switch placement) for a vehicle E/E architecture.
- The paper built on it (ITSC 2026, submission 1118) was rejected. Reviewers: model too simplified, parameters without source, thin evaluation, no baseline, AD/SDV framing inconsistent.
- A code audit found that the published "uncertainty" has no real effect: the latency constraint never binds, the whole vehicle fits one partition, and the only driver of the published topology difference is two redundancy pairs. The solver was not the bottleneck; the formulation was.
- The research question we answer (from the funding proposal, WP V.1-1): *"How can automotive E/E architectures be designed such that their contribution to uncertainties is minimized?"*
- "Contribution to uncertainty" is now defined as the **guaranteed envelope**: the set of scenarios (software growth, hardware degradation, single failures) within which an architecture keeps its resource, timing and availability guarantees. Wider envelope = less added uncertainty.

---

## 2. Binding decisions (D1–D7)

**D1 · Design time only.** The model synthesises architectures on the drawing board. Failure and growth scenarios are evaluated there. No runtime mechanism (no reconfiguration, no monitors) is modelled or claimed.

**D2 · Whole vehicle.** The object is the complete E/E architecture of a software-defined vehicle: ADAS/AD, body, comfort, powertrain, infotainment, connectivity. No function class is privileged in the model. Results are reported per function class.

**D3 · Stay with MILP; fix the formulation.**
- Partitions remain the unit of synthesis and are ASIL-homogeneous. Optional flag `allow_downward_asil_sharing`: a partition of ASIL *a* may host SWCs with ASIL ≤ *a* at an upgrade cost.
- Replace explicit partition slots `z[i,j,a,p]` with `x[i,j]` (placement) and integer `P[j,a]` (partition count).
- Per site: partition cap, capacity cap, fixed activation cost. ECUs still emerge from composition, but neither unlimited nor free.
- Replace per-flow link binaries `flow[t,u,v]` with candidate paths per site pair.
- Solver hygiene: tight variable bounds, symmetry breaking, `MIPGap = 0.01`, keep time-limited solutions (report gap), warm starts across the ε sweep, one model instance reused.
- Latency constraints in real units (ms), chain-based, able to bind.
- For large instances (≥ 12 sites, ≥ 150 units) use a hierarchy: cluster → solve → refine at SWC level. Do not attempt a flat model for 1,000+ SWCs.

**D4 · Added uncertainty = guaranteed envelope.** Three guarantees, all hard constraints in every scenario of the chosen set:
- Resource: demand ≤ capacity in every partition.
- Timing: end-to-end latency of every critical chain ≤ its bound (a degraded bound is allowed in failure scenarios).
- Availability: every critical function keeps running under every single failure.
Scenario dimensions: (a) software growth, domain-based profiles scaled by a scalar α; (b) single failures of every ECU site, every switch, every cable; (c) hardware degradation as deterministic per-site derating. There is no violation probability in the main method.

**D5 · Objectives.** Minimise cost; maximise the tolerated growth level α under all single failures. Pareto front in two dimensions (x = α, y = cost). Produce two curves: with and without failure constraints. Cable length and mass are reported, not optimised. The old model corresponds to the point α = 0 without failure constraints.

**D6 · Evaluation.** One evaluator script scores every design identically (optimiser output and hand-built templates). Outputs listed in section 8.

**D7 · Contributions.** C1 model (composition at physical sites with network, caps, fixed cost), C2 metric (guaranteed envelope as Pareto axis), C3 evaluation (synthesised vs. central/domain/zonal templates), C4 open code and measured parameters.

---

## 3. What is wrong in the current code (audit, for orientation)

File references are to `optimizer.py` and `main.py` at the time of the audit.

| Finding | Where | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Explicit identical partition slots `z[i,j,a,p]`, 3 per (site, ASIL) | `_create_model_and_variables`, ~l. 234–245 | Symmetric copies of every solution; paper describes `x + P` instead |
| Per-flow link binaries `flow[t,u,v]`: 2,700 of 4,287 variables (63%) | ~l. 444–450 | Blocks scaling; at 18 sites / 195 SWCs ≈ 2.5 M binaries |
| Big-M = 1000 on `comm ≤ M·loc_active`; `split_m = 1e6` | ~l. 785, 944, 1183–1219 | LP bound ≈ 13% below optimum; proof of optimality takes days |
| Latency constraint `c8` generates 0 constraints (per-metre latency 0.1, max distance 3 m, robust latency 0.33 < limit 1.0) | precompute + `c8_loc_loc_latency` | "Network uncertainty" has no effect on any result; paper's eq. (11) not implemented |
| `health_factor = 0.85` at every site | config | "Location-aware derating" does not differentiate sites |
| `enable_uncertainty` also toggles redundancy (anti-colocation) and contention constraints | ~l. 854–890 | The with/without comparison is confounded |
| Only 2 SWCs require `HW_ACC`, `K_THRESHOLD = 2` | config | Contention constraint never binds |
| Each ASIL group fits one partition; whole-vehicle CPU demand 84,731 vs. effective partition capacity 85,000 | config | Centralisation is a parameter effect; opening a site costs one partition per ASIL present |
| Time-limited solves are discarded even with an incumbent | Pareto loop, ~l. 1604–1606 | 3 of 5 Pareto points survived |
| `MIPGap` default (0.01%), `MIPFocus = 2`, `Method = 6`; `--mip_gap` and `--warm_start` parsed but unused | optimize(), `main.py` | Wrong settings for time-limited exploration |
| Model rebuilt from scratch per ε point; cable-optimal extreme not lexicographically optimised | Pareto loop | Slow; weakly Pareto-optimal point |
| Hard-coded constants: ASIL margins, `K_THRESHOLD`, `NETWORK_SAFETY_FACTOR 1.10`, `SYNC 0.0005`, `PLATFORM 0.002`, `SWITCH_ETH_PORTS 16`, `GRB_LICENSE_FILE` path | top of `optimizer.py` | Not configurable, no provenance |

Verified empirically: the exported model (LP file) solved with HiGHS in 240 s reaches 7,308 (gap 4.8%); with symmetry breaking on partition slots and `comm` upper bound `ceil(total_volume / capacity)` it reaches 7,200 (the paper's optimum, gap 3.2%).

---

## 4. Target model (v2)

### 4.1 Sets and parameters
- `S` software components (all domains), with: `domain`, `class` (AD, body, comfort, powertrain, infotainment, connectivity), `asil ∈ K`, `period_ms`, resource demands `R[i,c]` for `c ∈ {cpu, ram, rom}`, `hw_required ⊆ H`, `interfaces ⊆ I`, `critical ∈ {0,1}`, `redundant_with` (optional), `growth_domain`.
- `L` candidate sites, with coordinates, `thermal_class`, `cap_partitions[j]`, `cap_capacity[j,c]`, `fixed_cost[j]`, `derating[j] ∈ (0,1]`.
- `E_ch` harness channel graph (nodes incl. sites, sensors, actuators; edges with length). `Dist(u,v)` = shortest path on `E_ch`, precomputed.
- `H` hardware features (accelerator, HSM, DSP, switch) with cost; `I` interface types with port cost, cable cost per metre, mass per metre, capacity, per-hop latency.
- `T` data flows between SWCs and between sensors/actuators and SWCs: volume, period, `chain_id`.
- `C` cause-effect chains: ordered SWC list, `bound_ms`, `degraded_bound_ms`.
- `Paths(u,v)` candidate backbone paths per site pair (enumerate simple paths up to 2 intermediate sites; cap at k = 3–5 per pair).
- Growth profiles `g[d]` per growth domain (fraction at α = 1); scalar α.
- Failure set `F`: one scenario per site, per switch-equipped site (switch failure), per backbone link. Generated automatically.

### 4.2 Decision variables
- `x[i,j] ∈ {0,1}` SWC i at site j (nominal placement).
- `y[j] ∈ {0,1}` site active; `P[j,a] ∈ ℤ≥0` partitions of ASIL a at site j.
- `hw[j,h] ∈ {0,1}`; `port[j,ω] ∈ ℤ≥0`; `w[u,v,ω] ∈ ℤ≥0` wires per link and type.
- `sel[u,v,p] ∈ {0,1}` backbone path chosen for traffic between sites u and v; `f[u,v,p] ≥ 0` volume on that path.
- Static redundancy for critical SWCs: `r[i,j] ∈ {0,1}` redundant instance of i at site j (cost counted; needed for availability under failure). **Open:** confirm static redundancy as the availability mechanism (consistent with D1) rather than any re-placement.
- If `allow_downward_asil_sharing`: `u[i,j,a] ∈ {0,1}` SWC i hosted in a partition of ASIL a ≥ asil(i) at site j, with upgrade cost.

### 4.3 Constraints
1. Unique placement: `Σ_j x[i,j] = 1`. Redundant instance: `Σ_j r[i,j] = critical[i]` (or ≥ 1 for critical), `r[i,j] ≤ 1 − x[i,j]`.
2. Site activation and caps: `P[j,a] ≤ cap_partitions[j]·y[j]`; `Σ_a P[j,a]·C_part ≤ cap_capacity[j]·y[j]`; `x[i,j] ≤ y[j]`, `r[i,j] ≤ y[j]`.
3. Resource per (site, ASIL), nominal with growth and derating:
   `Σ_{i: asil(i)=a} (x[i,j] + r[i,j]) · R[i,c] · (1 + α·g[dom(i)]) ≤ P[j,a] · C_part[c] · derating[j]` for all j, a, c.
   With downward sharing: replace `asil(i) = a` by host-ASIL variables `u`.
4. Hardware dependencies: `x[i,j] ≤ hw[j,h]` and `r[i,j] ≤ hw[j,h]` for `h ∈ hw_required(i)`.
5. Ports: outgoing + incoming wires + peripheral connections ≤ `port[j,ω]`. Shared-bus segregation by criticality group as in v1 (keep).
6. Aggregated backbone traffic: `vol[u,v] = Σ_t volume_t · lnk[t,u,v]` with `lnk[t,u,v] ≥ x[src(t),u] + x[dst(t),v] − 1` (continuous, 0..1; also for redundant instances). `Σ_p f[u,v,p] = vol[u,v]`; `f[u,v,p] ≤ M_uv · sel[u,v,p]` with `M_uv = Σ_t volume_t`; `Σ_p sel[u,v,p] ≤ 1`.
7. Link capacity: `Σ_{(u,v,p) ∋ e} f[u,v,p] ≤ Σ_ω w[e,ω]·cap_ω` for every backbone link e; `w[e,ω] ≤ ceil(Σ_t volume_t / cap_ω)` (tight bound).
8. Switching: a path with an intermediate site requires `hw[site, switch] = 1` (link `sel` to `hw`).
9. Timing: for each chain c, `lat_c = Σ_{i∈c} exec[i, site] + Σ_{hops on chosen paths} (per_hop_latency + serialisation) + sync_penalty · (number of site boundaries crossed) ≤ bound_ms[c]`. Linearise via `lnk` and `sel`. Must be able to bind with measured values.
10. Redundancy independence: for each critical i, `x[i,j] + r[i,j] ≤ 1` (different site); primary and redundant paths to the same sensor sources must not share a switch or a backbone link (disjointness via `sel` on paths that share elements).
11. Failure scenarios (hard, for every f ∈ F): for each critical function i, at least one instance (primary or redundant) is on a surviving site and its chosen paths avoid the failed element. Implement by precomputing, per path, the set of elements it uses; a scenario f removes those paths and that site; the constraint reads `Σ_j surv_f(j)·(x[i,j]·pathok_f + r[i,j]·pathok_f) ≥ 1`, linearised with the `sel` variables. Non-critical functions may be lost (degraded mode).
12. Timing in failure scenarios: chain latency ≤ `degraded_bound_ms` using surviving instances.

### 4.4 Objective and sweep
- Cost: `Σ_j fixed_cost[j]·y[j] + Σ_{j,a} P[j,a]·cost_part + Σ hw·cost_h + Σ port·cost_ω + Σ_e,ω w[e,ω]·Dist(e)·cost_ω + Σ upgrade costs`. Cable cost at realistic €/m.
- Sweep: for α in a configured list (e.g. 0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0): solve min cost with all failure constraints; repeat without failure constraints. Record cost, gap, solve time, design. Warm-start each α from the previous solution.
- Reported per design: cable length, cable mass, active sites, partitions per site, archetype label (rule in 8.2), per-class placement.

### 4.5 Symmetry and bounds
- If identical sites exist (mirror left/right), add ordering constraints on `y`.
- All integer variables get explicit upper bounds derived from data.
- Keep `MIPFocus` default or 1; `Method` default; `MIPGap = 0.01`; `TimeLimit` per solve configurable; accept `SolCount > 0` on `TIME_LIMIT` and log the gap.

---

## 5. Scenario generation

- **Growth:** `growth_profiles.json` maps growth domains to fractions at α = 1 (e.g. AD +30%, infotainment +50%, body +5%). α scales all fractions. Profiles carry a `source` field each.
- **Single failures:** generated from the layout: each site, each site with a switch (switch-only failure), each backbone link. One scenario per element.
- **Degradation:** `derating[j]` from the site's thermal class, deterministic. (Open: randomised derating in evaluation only, later.)
- **Critical set:** `critical[i]` from config. (Open: rule to be defined; default proposal ASIL ≥ B, to be confirmed.)

---

## 6. Evaluator (independent of the optimiser)

Input: an architecture description (sites active, partitions, hardware, ports, wires, placement, redundant instances, chosen paths) plus the same config. Output:
- Guarantee checks for a given α and scenario: resource, timing, availability (booleans with slack values).
- Envelope α*: maximum α (bisection over a grid) such that all three guarantees hold in the nominal scenario and in every single-failure scenario.
- Cost, cable length, cable mass, per-class placement summary, archetype label, coverage table (scenario × what survives / what degrades).
The evaluator must be the only scorer used for both optimiser outputs and templates.

**Templates** (`templates/`): hand-built central (one compute site + minimal switches), domain (one controller per class), zonal (one controller per zone + one central compute) architectures on the same layout, placed by simple rules; scored with the evaluator; their α* and cost plotted with the Pareto fronts.

---

## 7. Data and configuration (schema v3)

All parameters move out of code into JSON with a `source` field per value: `measured | datasheet | literature | assumption`. A script compiles the provenance table.

- `swc.json`: `id, domain, class, asil, period_ms, cpu, ram, rom, hw_required[], interfaces[], critical, redundant_with, growth_domain, position (for sensors/actuators), source`.
- `chains.json`: `id, swcs[], bound_ms, degraded_bound_ms, source`.
- `flows.json`: `src, dst, volume_kbps, period_ms, chain_id, source` (AD part measured with `ros2 topic bw/hz`, CARET for latencies).
- `sites.json`: `id, x, y, thermal_class, cap_partitions, cap_capacity{cpu,ram,rom}, fixed_cost, cooling, source`.
- `channels.json`: nodes and edges with lengths (harness channel graph).
- `hardware.json`: partition capacity and cost; feature costs; port costs; cable types with cost per metre, mass per metre, capacity, per-hop latency, sync accuracy class.
- `growth_profiles.json`, `scenarios.json` (which failure classes to include, derating per thermal class).
- Non-AD function set: 100–200 functions across body, comfort, powertrain, infotainment, connectivity, each with the fields above and a literature source (AUTOSAR domain structure, Reger 2017, E/E literature).

Target sizes: 10–20 candidate sites; AD part from measured Autoware clusters with an explicit clustering rule; granularity sensitivity at two or three cluster counts.

---

## 8. Experiments and outputs

### 8.1 Figures and tables
1. Pareto front cost vs. α, with failure constraints; second curve without. Templates as points. Old model as the α = 0 / no-failure point.
2. Archetype label per Pareto point.
3. Scenario coverage table (design × failure scenario).
4. Ablation: toggle failure constraints, timing constraints, downward ASIL sharing, site caps/fixed cost; report topology and cost changes.
5. Sensitivity and tipping points: fixed site cost, partition cost, cable €/m, capacity caps; the parameter values where the archetype flips.
6. Scalability: 6, 8, 12, 18 sites; solve time, model size, gap; hierarchy beyond.
7. Per function class results.
8. Harness: cable length and mass per design.
9. Provenance table.

### 8.2 Archetype labelling rule (initial)
- Central: ≥ 80% of compute demand on one site.
- Zonal: ≥ 3 active sites each hosting peripherals of its own zone, plus at most one central compute site.
- Domain: sites host functions mostly of one class each.
- Hybrid otherwise. (Open: refine after first results.)

---

## 9. Implementation order and acceptance criteria

1. **Vehicle layout.** Deliver `sites.json` (10–20 sites, coordinates, thermal classes), sensor/body-load positions, `channels.json`. Acceptance: distances computed from the channel graph; a plotted layout.
2. **Visualisation on the layout.** Physical view (sites, links by type, utilisation as line width, legend, no numbers on lines), logical topology view, summary table per solution. Acceptance: all three render for any evaluator-readable design.
3. **Code hygiene on the current model.** Solver settings; keep time-limited solutions; symmetry breaking; tight `comm` bounds; warm starts; split `enable_uncertainty` into five flags; constants into config; remove hard-coded licence path. Acceptance: 65×6 instance solves to 1% gap in minutes; a reproducible run script.
4. **Go / no-go test.** On the current model add one switch-failure scenario and one growth profile. Acceptance: a written answer to "does the hardware decision change?" with the two designs plotted.
5. **Data, AD side.** Autoware measurement via rosbag replay; per-node resources, topic bw/hz, CARET latencies; clustering rule. Acceptance: `swc.json` AD part with `source: measured`.
6. **Data, non-AD side.** Sourced function set; redundancy pairs; critical set defined. Acceptance: provenance for every function.
7. **Parameters.** Capacities, caps, costs, cable cost/mass, thermal classes, growth profiles. Acceptance: provenance table generated.
8. **Model v2** (section 4). Acceptance: unit tests on constraint generation; v1 results reproduced as the α = 0 / no-failure case; 18-site instance solves within a configured time limit with a reported gap.
9. **Evaluator and templates** (section 6). Acceptance: evaluator reproduces the optimiser's own constraint checks on its outputs; templates scored.
10. **Experiments** (section 8). Acceptance: every figure produced by a script from saved results; no manual numbers.
11. **Writing.** Not part of this handoff.

Steps 5–7 can run in parallel with 8. Step 10 must not start before 9.

---

## 10. Non-goals

- No runtime reconfiguration, monitors or mitigation logic.
- No closed-loop driving simulation (nuPlan) and no latency-to-position-error conversion.
- No real-vehicle or HIL validation claims.
- No probabilistic/Monte Carlo violation metric in the main pipeline (may be added later as a stress test).
- Do not replace the MILP with a metaheuristic or a learned method; use hierarchy for scale.

---

## 11. Open items (need a human decision before coding the affected part)

1. Availability mechanism under failure: static redundancy (`r[i,j]`) as specified, or something else.
2. Critical function set rule (default proposal: ASIL ≥ B).
3. Growth profile values and their sources.
4. Degraded timing bounds per chain.
5. Archetype labelling thresholds.
6. Randomised degradation in evaluation (later).

---

## 12. Conventions

- Python, Gurobi via gurobipy; all parameters from JSON config; no magic numbers in code.
- Every solve logs: status, objective, bound, gap, time, variable/constraint counts.
- Results saved as JSON per design; figures generated from saved results only.
- Tests: constraint-generation unit tests on a tiny instance (3 sites, 10 SWCs) with hand-checked expected values.
- Keep v1 runnable behind a flag for the baseline point.

---

## 13. Future work

Items deferred from step 3 ("code hygiene") in section 9, to be discussed before implementation.

### Reuse one model across the Pareto sweep (warm start)

Currently every Pareto point rebuilds the Gurobi model from scratch and solves it without a starting solution.
Proposal: build the model once, change only the objective and the ε-constraint right-hand side per point, and pass the previous point's solution as a MIP start.

Open question: this may change results.
- If a point has several optimal designs with equal cost, the warm start can steer Gurobi to a different one than a cold start.
- With a MIP gap > 0 or a time limit, the returned design and objective can differ.
Before adopting, compare warm- and cold-started runs on the baseline instances (jobs 241 and 242) and decide whether the difference is acceptable.

---

## 14. Literature

Selection rule: peer-reviewed venues only (IEEE/ACM transactions and conferences, SAE, Elsevier, Springer). "Read" states whether the full text or only the abstract was checked.

### 14.1 Harness routing on a channel graph (relevant to step 1)

**C.-W. Lin, L. Rao, P. Giusto, J. G. D'Ambrosio, A. L. Sangiovanni-Vincentelli, "An Efficient Wire Routing and Wire Sizing Algorithm for Weight Minimization of Automotive Systems", DAC 2014.**
DOI [10.1145/2593069.2593088](https://doi.org/10.1145/2593069.2593088), open access: [eScholarship](https://escholarship.org/uc/item/2pv747kz). Read: full text.
Extended journal version: IEEE TCAD 2015, DOI [10.1109/TCAD.2015.2448680](https://doi.org/10.1109/TCAD.2015.2448680). Read: not yet (paywalled).
- Vehicle model: a given *routing graph* of the harness. Vertices are *locations* (points where wires enter or leave a harness; splices allowed, with a capacity), *parts* (ECU, sensor, actuator; degree 1, no pass-through) and *inlines* (connectors between two harnesses). Edges carry lengths; at least one end of every edge is a location.
- Each wire set (netlist) is routed as a Steiner tree that must be a subgraph of the routing graph; splices only at locations. Objective: total wire weight, segment weight ∝ L·r², with a resistance bound ∝ L/r² per netlist.
- Method: modified KMB Steiner heuristic, LP/MILP splice relocation (CPLEX 12.5), wire sizing heuristic.
- Industrial case (GM; object detection, exterior lighting, mirror and window control): 17 harnesses, 102 locations, 20 inlines, 248 parts, 387 edges, 100 netlists. Result: total length 285,010 mm (> 4 % shorter than the designers' semi-automated solution), total weight 2.707 kg (> 10 % lighter).
- Motivation figures quoted in the paper (secondary, from its references): wiring weight up to 30 kg; wiring and harness are the third heaviest and costliest component after chassis and engine.

Implications for us:
- Industrial practice routes wires only along a predefined harness routing graph. This supports computing all distances as shortest paths on a channel graph (`channels.json`) instead of the current Manhattan distance.
- `channels.json` can reuse their vertex types: ECU sites, sensors and actuators as parts; harness branch points as locations; pass-throughs (firewall, doors) as inlines.
- Their graph is proprietary and not published. We build ours from the vehicle geometry and standard routing practice, and must cite a source for that practice (still to be found, e.g. an automotive electrics handbook).
- The weight model (∝ L·r²) can be used for the harness mass that D5 says we report but do not optimise.

### 14.2 Motivation: centralised E/E architectures

**V. Bandur, G. Selim, V. Pantelic, M. Lawford, "Making the Case for Centralized Automotive E/E Architectures", IEEE Transactions on Vehicular Technology, 2021.**
DOI [10.1109/TVT.2021.3054934](https://doi.org/10.1109/TVT.2021.3054934). Read: abstract only (paywalled).
- Compares the drawbacks of decentralised architectures with the benefits of centralisation; reviews enabling technologies (in-vehicle networking, virtualisation, ECU hardware, AUTOSAR); functional safety as the overarching concern.
- Use: motivation and related work. No physical layout data.

**L. Mauser, S. Wagner, "Centralization potential of automotive E/E architectures", Journal of Systems and Software, 2024.**
DOI [10.1016/j.jss.2024.112220](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jss.2024.112220), open access: [arXiv 2409.10690](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.10690). Read: abstract only.
- Seven properties where current architectures reach their limits: busload, functional safety, computing power, feature dependencies, development and maintenance cost, error rate, modularity/flexibility. Based on a literature review and practitioner interviews; centralisation can mitigate these limits, with guidance to avoid a monolithic design.
- Use: several properties map onto our metrics (busload ↔ link utilisation, functional safety ↔ ASIL and redundancy constraints, modularity/flexibility ↔ growth envelope α).

---

### 14.3 Timing model for B4 (literature, 2026-10-19)

Peer-reviewed sources read for the latency model (full texts of the first three, abstract page of the fourth):

- **Rox, Ernst, Giusto, "Using Timing Analysis for the Design of Future Switched Based Ethernet Automotive Networks", DATE 2012.** Full-duplex switched Ethernet: the transmission delay of a frame equals its size in bytes (payload + protocol overhead) divided by the bit rate (Sec. II); crossing a switch adds (1) store-and-forward (again size / bit rate), (2) switch-fabric processing (assumed 0–2 µs), (3) wire-line delay (negligible), (4) queueing (bounded with compositional performance analysis, static-priority non-preemptive per output port). End-to-end latency = sum over hops. Example at 100 Mbit/s: CAN-over-UDP frame 102 B = 8.16 µs, FlexRay frame 326 B = 26.08 µs, 1542 B video/audio frame = 123.36 µs per link; worst-case end-to-end over the backbone 2.3 ms (CAN frames) to 10–14 ms (video, wireless) without shaping (Tables IV–V).
- **Lin, Zhu, Phung, Sangiovanni-Vincentelli, "Security-Aware Mapping for CAN-Based Real-Time Distributed Automotive Systems", ICCAD 2013** (MILP mapping of tasks to ECUs on CAN). End-to-end latency of a functional path (eq. 1): sum of the worst-case response times of all tasks on the path plus, for every *global* signal (sender and receiver on different ECUs), the message response time plus the periods of the signal and of its destination task (asynchronous sampling). Local signals (same ECU) add nothing — the same crossing structure as ours. Message response times from CAN schedulability analysis.
- **Thiele, Schlatow, Axer, Ernst, "Formal timing analysis of CAN-to-Ethernet gateway strategies in automotive networks", Real-Time Systems 52, 88–112, 2016 (DOI 10.1007/s11241-015-9243-y).** Path latency = sum of worst-case response times of the tasks on the path; CAN bus response times from Davis et al. 2007; Ethernet's own contribution negligible next to gateway multiplexing delays; example: 100 Mbit/s links, 500 kbit/s CAN, gateway execution 10–50 µs.
- **Davis, Burns, Bril, Lukkien, "Controller Area Network (CAN) schedulability analysis: Refuted, revisited and revised", Real-Time Systems 35(3), 239–272, 2007.** Worst-case response time of a CAN message = queueing delay (blocking + higher-priority interference) + transmission time (frame bits / bus rate). The rigorous bound for a CAN hop; its result is what our per-type hop-latency parameter has to upper-bound.
- **Teper, Günzel, Ueter, von der Brüggen, Chen, "End-To-End Timing Analysis in ROS2", RTSS 2022** and **Casini, Blaß, Lütkebohle, Brandenburg, "Response-Time Analysis of ROS 2 Processing Chains Under Reservation-Based Scheduling", ECRTS 2019.** Cause-effect chains sensor → components → actuator; metrics maximum reaction time and maximum data age; chain latency bounded by summing per-component response times along the chain (single ECU in RTSS 2022).
- **Askaripoor, Mueller, Knoll, "E/E Designer: A Framework to Design and Synthesize Vehicle E/E Architecture", IEEE T-IV 9(7), 5204–5221, 2024.** Synthesis with MIP that maps software to hardware, routes messages and schedules communication; cited as the closest synthesis work with network timing (full text not read).

What B4 takes from this: (1) per crossing, latency = hops × (message size / link rate + per-hop processing/queueing bound) is the standard decomposition (Rox 2012, Thiele 2016); (2) the chain sum with "no term for same-ECU neighbours" is exactly Lin 2013 eq. (1); Lin additionally adds sampling periods (asynchronous communication) — we omit periods for lack of period data and keep a per-crossing synchronisation constant; (3) queueing/arbitration enters as a per-type hop bound (CAN: Davis 2007 response time; Ethernet: CPA bound), i.e. a parameter of the cable type, not a decision; (4) chain bounds are stated as maximum reaction time of the chain (RTSS 2022 terminology). Industry figures seen but not peer-reviewed (not cited): OEM requirement of ≤ 14 µs forwarding delay per automotive Ethernet switch (Keysight application note).

---

### 14.4 Literature for the uncertainty envelope (step C, 2026-10-20)

Peer-reviewed sources behind the C decisions (abstracts/metadata read; full texts where open):

- **Becker, Schätz, Armbruster, Buckl, "A Formal Model for Constraint-Based Deployment Calculation and Analysis for Fault-Tolerant Systems", SEFM 2014, LNCS 8702 (DOI 10.1007/978-3-319-10431-7_15)** (preprint: arXiv 1404.7763, workshop version EDSoS 2014). Constraint-based deployment of mixed-critical software so that selected components stay fail-operational; a second instance is deployed as hot or cold standby, and the choice is decided by the component's minimum fault-tolerant time against the platform's fault-recovery time (their target switch-over 50 ms). → Our rule: hot standby when the chain's fault-tolerant time is shorter than the recovery time, otherwise the optimiser may choose cold standby.
- **Kohn, Käßmeyer, Schneider, Roth, Fruechtl, Knoll, "Fail-operational in safety-related automotive multi-core systems", IEEE SIES 2015, pp. 144–147.** Overview of fail-operational mechanisms on multi-core domain controllers (Audi/Infineon/Elektrobit) — redundancy patterns and degradation.
- **Drago, Bondavalli, Lollini, Antlanger, Drenkelforth, "Model-based dependability assessment of fault-tolerant architectures for autonomous driving systems", Journal of Systems and Software, 2026 (DOI 10.1016/j.jss.2026.112972)** and **Drago et al., "Quantitative Comparison of System Architectures for an SAE Level 4 Highway Pilot", QRS 2025 (DOI 10.1109/QRS65678.2025.00066).** Monolithic (single computing channel) vs symmetric vs asymmetric redundant architectures for L4; monolithic designs rarely acceptable; trade-offs between the redundancy architectures quantified by model-based dependability analysis. (Full text paywalled; cited from metadata and the open abstract.)
- **Glaß, Lukasiewycz, Streichert, Haubelt, Teich, "Reliability-Aware System Synthesis", DATE 2007, pp. 409–414.** Reliability as a design objective during system synthesis instead of adding redundancy afterwards; redundant bindings of tasks. **Reimann, Glaß, Lukasiewycz, Haubelt, Keinert, Teich, "Symbolic voter placement for dependability-aware system synthesis", CODES+ISSS 2008**: redundant task binding plus voter placement. **Streichert, Glaß, Haubelt, Teich, "Design space exploration of reliable networked embedded systems", Journal of Systems Architecture 53(10), 751–763, 2007 (DOI 10.1016/j.sysarc.2007.01.005)**: topology optimisation where both computational nodes and communication links are allocated and data dependencies can be routed over several routes for reliability (automotive case study). → Failure classes node, switch and link, and disjoint routes for redundant instances.
- **Ebert, Jones, "Embedded Software: Facts, Figures, and Future", IEEE Computer 42(4), 2009 (DOI 10.1109/MC.2009.118).** Volume of embedded software growing by 10–20 % per year "depending on the domain". **Broy, Krüger, Pretschner, Salzmann, "Engineering Automotive Software", Proceedings of the IEEE 95(2), 356–373, 2007 (DOI 10.1109/JPROC.2006.888386).** Growth of automotive software driven by cheaper hardware and new functionality. → Growth as a scalar level α with domain-specific profiles g[d]; the profile values are our scenario parameters (sensitivity study), the direction (innovation-driven domains grow faster) comes from these sources.
- Not peer-reviewed, indicative only (not cited in the paper): Bosch fact sheet 10 M lines (2010) → ~100 M today → 300–500 M for automated vehicles; NXP/Wards survey on processing headroom (30–50 % caps by 2030); PREEvision article (section 15.4).

---

## 15. Reference vehicle (decision 2026-10-16)

**Vehicle: Hyundai IONIQ 5** (E-GMP skateboard platform). Rationale: the sensor suite used in the paper is the nuPlan suite; nuPlan was recorded on Chrysler Pacifica PHEVs, but Motional's production robotaxi built on the same stack is the IONIQ 5. A lidar-equipped series car with a different suite (Lotus Eletre) was rejected to keep the sensor set unchanged.

Sources:
- nuPlan sensor suite: 5 lidars (Pandar 40P on the roof and on both A-pillars, Pandar 20PA/20PB in the front/rear bumper), 8 cameras (D3 Engineering D3RCM), GNSS, IMU; fleet of 31 Chrysler Pacifica PHEVs. Karnchanachari et al., "Towards learning-based planning: The nuPlan benchmark for real-world autonomous driving", [arXiv:2403.04133](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.04133), 2024. Sensor extrinsics (translation/rotation w.r.t. the ego frame) are in the `camera` and `lidar` tables of the nuPlan database ([schema](https://github.com/motional/nuplan-devkit/blob/master/docs/nuplan_schema.md)).
- Motional IONIQ 5 robotaxi (30+ sensors; exact layout not published): [Hyundai/Motional press release, 2021-08-31](https://www.hyundai.news/eu/articles/press-releases/motional-and-hyundai-motor-group-unveil-the-ioniq-5-robotaxi.html); [Motional, "Building our robotaxi in less than a year"](https://motional.com/news/building-our-robotaxi-in-less-than-a-year).
- IONIQ 5 dimensions: 4,635 × 1,890 × 1,605 mm, wheelbase 3,000 mm; battery under the floor between the axles, motors on the axles (E-GMP). Official Hyundai technical data sheet still to be located for the standard model; the IONIQ 5 N sheet ([Hyundai Motor Europe, 2023](https://www.hyundai.news/newsroom/dam/eu/models/20231122_ioniq_5_n_sets_benchmark_for_sporty_evs/hyundai-ioniq-5-n-technical-data-1223-v4.pdf)) gives 4,715 × 1,940 × 1,585 mm.
- Exterior shell for visualisation: "2024_hyundai_ioniq_5_n" by tangwang920, Sketchfab, CC BY 4.0, [link](https://sketchfab.com/3d-models/2024-hyundai-ioniq-5-n-2e62fa6cd7814178acf49df42bb4ff24). Shell only; battery, motors, seats, pillars and cable channels are modelled as parametric blocks from the dimensions above (industry layout knowledge, marked as such in `source`).
- Rejected: PrintYourVehicle meshes (extracted from Forza Horizon 6; the site's "Research Use Notice" is a disclaimer, not a licence).

Open: nuPlan sensor extrinsics must be read from a nuPlan log (mini split suffices) and scaled from Pacifica to IONIQ 5 dimensions; the transfer rule is to be documented.

### 15.1 Reference measurements (IONIQ 5 N, private reference model, 2026-10-16)

Read headless from the PrintYourVehicle `.blend` (game-extracted; used only as a measuring reference, never redistributed or shown). Model units are metres, 1:1 for the body; z = 0 is the underbody (ground ≈ z − 0.16), y is longitudinal (front negative), x lateral. **Wheel meshes are not at their true positions** (wheelbase from wheel meshes 2.60 m, from the wheel arches 2.95 m ≈ spec 3.00 m); use arches and spec values instead.

| Feature | Model coordinates (m) | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Body envelope (incl. mirrors) | x ±1.09, y −2.46…2.34, z 0…1.44 | scale check vs spec 4.715 × 1.940 × 1.585 |
| Front wheel arch / rear arch centre | y ≈ −1.45 / ≈ 1.50 | axle positions, motor blocks |
| Front motor bay (engineBay) | y −2.27…−1.06, z 0.07…0.86 | frunk / front e-motor + candidate site |
| Cabin floor (carpet) | y −1.08…1.13, z 0.15 (top ≈ 0.20 at sills) | floor level; battery below floor |
| Door sill (rocker) line | z 0.18…0.23, x ±0.80 | left/right sill harness channel |
| A / B / C pillar (door jambs) | y ≈ −0.98 / 0.20 / 1.42, x ±0.63…0.93 | pillar channels, A-pillar lidars, B-pillar sites |
| Dashboard (dashStructure) | y −1.28…−0.63, z 0.46…0.90 | cross-car beam channel, dash sites |
| Centre console | y −1.09…0.21, x ±0.13, z 0.14…0.59 | tunnel channel, console site |
| Rear seats | y 0.47…1.28, z 0.21…1.06 | under-seat sites |
| Trunk bay | y 1.10…2.19, z 0.49…1.36 | trunk sites, rear cross channel |
| Windscreen / rear glass | y −1.37…−0.39, z 0.81…1.29 / y 1.45…2.09, z 0.94…1.25 | roof-edge lidar positions |
| Headliner (overheadConsole) | z ≈ 1.20…1.27 | roof channel height |
| Headlights / taillights | y ≈ −2.1, z 0.50…0.63 / y ≈ 2.2, z 0.73…0.79 | corner radar / bumper lidar heights |

### 15.2 Cable routing space (implemented 2026-10-16)

Hand-drawn channel polylines were replaced by a routing space derived from the car geometry (`tools/vehicle_layout/routing_space.py`, runs inside Blender):
- The body envelope is voxelised (6 cm). A voxel is routable when it lies on the inner side of the nearest structure surface at 2–10 cm distance (the cavity between structure and trim where harnesses run: behind the body skin incl. roof and rockers, inside the pillars, under the floor carpet, inside the dashboard, along the tunnel, behind the trunk and engine-bay walls), is inside the envelope and not inside an obstacle block (battery, motors, wheel wells, seats). Result: 6,218 routable voxels in one connected component (3 stray voxels dropped). Stored in `configs/routing_space.npz` (git-ignored: derived from the private reference model; for the public release regenerate it from the CC-BY shell plus the block model).
- `generate_layout.py` builds the routing graph from the voxels (edges between voxels up to 2 voxels apart, Euclidean length), attaches every site / sensor / actuator to its nearest routable voxel (drop), and computes all-pairs shortest cable lengths (`configs/distances.json`). Mean site–site cable length 2.34 m (hand channels: 3.37 m; Manhattan in v1: n/a).
- `configs/channels.json` now holds only a harness *backbone* for visualisation: the union of the shortest paths of a minimum spanning tree over all 48 entities (177 nodes / 176 edges). The union of all pairwise shortest paths (3,087 edges) was tried first and is unreadable (diagonal paths across the roof cavity).
- Candidate sites remain grid points of the hand-defined permissible volumes (`ecu_zones`); three zones were moved inboard after a clearance check against the structure meshes.
- Open: shortest paths through the roof cavity run diagonally; real harnesses follow rails/bows. Option: edge weights per kind (penalise 'body' cavity vs. pillars/sills/tunnel) or restrict the roof band to a strip along the rails.

### 15.3 Layout visualisation (status 2026-10-16)

`tools/vehicle_viz/build_vehicle.py` (Blender 5.2 bpy + PIL compositing, run via Slurm) renders the layout inside the reference car model: transparent shell, subdued interior, wheels corrected to the arch positions and the real tyre radius, battery/motor blocks, candidate sites as numbered badges (1–26), sensors/actuators as codes (C1–C8, L1–L5, I, G, A1–A7), harness backbone coloured by routing kind, legend table (`legend.png`/`legend.md`). Views: iso, top (front left), side; `--band` overlays the voxelised cable space; `--labels names|numbers|none`. Outputs under `results/vehicle_layout/final/`. Step 1 acceptance (distances from the channel graph, plotted layout) is met. The three style drafts of the judged panel were deleted after merging their best features into the script.
- Engine bay (added 2026-10-16, after review): the reference model contains the front drive unit, inverter, reservoirs and covers (31 `engineBay_a`/`engine_a` parts). These are now (a) obstacles for the cable band (only the four bay wall/liner meshes count as 'bay' structure) and (b) the basis for the engine-bay ECU candidates: `tools/vehicle_layout/bay_candidates.py` samples the bay on a 3 cm grid, computes the clearance to all solid parts, keeps enclosed points outside the components and returns the clearance maxima (≥ 9 cm free radius, ≥ 0.35 m apart). The six chosen points (top of the drive unit, cowl left/right, strut towers left/right, front-right low) replaced the hand-drawn `front_bay_*`/`frunk` zones, three of which had been inside components. The front motor block is kept only as a routing obstacle (`draw: false`); the rear drive unit is not in the game model and stays a block. Drivetrain assumption: AWD (two motors) as in the IONIQ 5 N; the standard IONIQ 5 RWD has only the rear motor.
- Roof cavity (2026-10-16): the reference model has only the roof skin, so the band covered the whole headliner and shortest paths crossed it diagonally. `vehicle.json` → `roof_structure` now restricts the cavity above z = 1.42 m to two side rails (|x| ≥ 0.60), a centre line (|x| ≤ 0.10) and four transverse bows (y = 0.40, −0.20, −0.85, −1.42; A/B/C pillars + one mid bow), kind 'roof' (453 voxels removed; 5,703 routable). Drivetrain decision: AWD (two motors).
- Installation space (2026-10-16, replaces the hand-defined ECU zones): `routing_space.py` now scans one geometry and yields both spaces on a symmetric 6 cm grid. ECU space = points where a free sphere of radius ≥ 8 cm fits (clearance to all parts, blocks, glass), enclosed by the car (surfaces within 1.5 m in five directions), mountable (≤ 15 cm from a structure surface on its back side, or ≤ 12 cm above the floor carpet / trunk floor) and outside the seat volumes. Candidate sites = clearance maxima of that space at least `site_spacing` (0.30 m) apart: 60 sites (8 engine bay, 23 cabin, 29 trunk; 0 roof and 0 underbody because nothing ≥ 8 cm fits there), named S01…S60, thermal class by region. `ecu_zones` in vehicle.json are a fallback without the reference model only; `bay_candidates.py` was folded into the scan. The asymmetric grid of the first version (left/right attachments differed) is fixed. Door cards were added as structure ('door' kind) so door harnesses can be routed.
- Peripheral positions (2026-10-16): `routing_space.py --snap_peripherals` moves every exterior device (cameras, lidars, GPS, lamps) onto the nearest point of the car skin of the reference model, 1 cm inside (moves of 0.02–0.26 m from the hand-placed values; `snapped` field in sensors.json / actuators.json). Drops to the cable band are now ≤ 0.10 m for all exterior sensors and are hidden in the figures by default (`--show_drops`). Battery block raised to z 0.18–0.33 m (flush with the underbody) and shortened to y ±0.95 m (0.3 m clear of the drive units); 62 candidate sites after the rescan.
- Blocks (2026-10-16, after review): front motor drawn again (orange, like the rear one); auxiliary units added as obstacle blocks (kind 'aux': HVAC box behind the centre dashboard, 12 V battery in the rear-left bay corner, ICCU/on-board charger under the trunk floor; all assumptions). Lamps are no longer snapped to the skin (lamp control sits inside behind the lens); steering actuator = rack on the front subframe below the drive unit. 52 candidate sites after the rescan. The `.blend` now always contains the scanned cable space as a hidden collection 'Cable space' (toggle in Blender; rendered with --band).

### Blender MCP for interactive inspection (future work, 2026-10-19)

Today the layout scene is produced headless on the HPC (`build_vehicle.py`) and inspected by opening the `.blend` on the user's PC. A Blender MCP server (e.g. the open-source blender-mcp add-on) inside the user's Blender would let the assistant query and tweak the open scene live. Prerequisites: Blender 5.2 + MCP add-on on the PC, a reverse SSH tunnel from the PC to the HPC login node (TCP works through Warpgate), and the MCP server registered in Claude Code. Rule to keep reproducibility: MCP is for looking and trying only; every accepted change is written to `configs/vehicle.json` / the scripts and the scene is regenerated from them. Not set up yet.
- Doors (2026-10-19): door cards removed from the cable space again. A door harness only serves that door's own devices; the main harness never transits a door, and with door voxels connected to the pillars the shortest paths had used doors as sill-to-roof shortcuts. Re-add per-door sub-harnesses only if door devices (window, mirror, lock, speaker) enter the function set.
- ECU space (2026-10-19, replaces picked candidate sites): no points are selected any more. The ECU space (clearance ≥ 8 cm, mountable, enclosed, outside seats) is sampled on a 12 cm lattice and EVERY feasible cell (currently 90: 11 engine bay, 16 cabin, 63 trunk) is handed to the optimiser as a possible ECU position, exactly like the cable voxels for routing. The figures show the ECU space as a translucent green region ('ECU space' collection); ECUs are drawn only from an optimisation result. `--show_sites` draws the individual cells. `--ecu_spacing` controls the lattice (model size).
- Cable-space connectivity (2026-10-19, found by the user in Blender): the A- and C-pillar bands did not reach the roof band: the roof edge band lies at z 1.33–1.39 (curved roof corner) below the roof threshold, and there are no header beams above the windscreen / rear window in the reference model (glass only). Fixes in `roof_structure`: z_min 1.42 → 1.30, two virtual header beams (y = 0.36 and −1.40, z 1.33–1.48, 368 voxels) and a one-voxel gap-closing step in the band (708 voxels). Verified with touching-voxel paths: A-pillar top → roof centre 0.74 m, C-pillar top → roof 0.79 m (before: 4.1 m via the floor); 99 % of the band is one contiguous piece, remaining fragments ≤ 32 voxels. Front bumper connects to the dashboard through the bay walls / inner fenders (2.7 m), not through the floor.
- Live inspection: Blender MCP add-on (mcp-for-blender 2.2) on the PC, reverse SSH tunnel to login1:9876, server in `~/venvs/blender-mcp`, project `.mcp.json` (git-ignored). Verified by adding/removing a test object in the live scene.
- One installation space (2026-10-19): the separate cable band and ECU scan were merged. `vehicle.json` → `installation_space`: every mountable voxel (behind a structure surface within `mount_reach` 0.15 m or ≤ 0.12 m above the floor carpet / trunk floor, enclosed by the car, outside blocks, components and seats) gets a clearance = radius of the largest free sphere. Cables may use voxels with clearance ≥ cable_bundle_diameter/2 (15 mm); control units may be placed where clearance ≥ half the middle dimension of `ecu_size` (one size = the largest unit, 300 × 200 × 60 mm → 100 mm). Thresholds are parameters; the clearance map is stored, so they can be changed without rescanning. Result: 6,470 mountable voxels, 7,284 cable voxels after headers and gap closing (one piece), 219 ECU voxels → 33 lattice cells (8 engine bay, 3 cabin, 22 trunk). A single ECU size was chosen deliberately: the ECU's size is an output of the partition model, so the largest unit must fit everywhere a site is offered; a partition-count-dependent clearance constraint can be added later without rescanning.

## 16. Optimiser plan and status (2026-10-19)

Plan agreed with the user: A) run the v1 model on the new vehicle data end to end (sanity), B) reformulate (D3: x[i,j] + P[j,a], candidate paths from the voxel graph, site caps / fixed cost, latency in ms), C) uncertainty envelope (D4/D5: growth α, single failures, static redundancy; Pareto cost vs α; go/no-go test = first small instance), D) evaluator, templates, experiments.

A done (job 367, 65 SCs, 10 of the 33 ECU cells, 600 s per solve, all uncertainty flags): min-cost point optimal at 8,285 (3 ECUs: footwell left with 52 SCs incl. HW_ACC/DSP, trunk right, engine bay; 6 partitions; 24 cables, 39.2 m routed). Pipeline additions: `tools/vehicle_layout/solution_paths.py` routes every cable of a solution through the voxel graph (`<solution>_cables.json`), `build_vehicle.py --solution` draws ECU boxes at the used cells and the cables coloured by interface (results/vehicle_layout/solution_367/). Findings for B: (1) v1 with 10 cells = 10.8k variables, 4 min per solve; 33 cells is out of reach without the reformulation; (2) when the second lexicographic phase is skipped by the time limit, the min-cable point keeps arbitrary cost (41k: hardware at unused sites, 33 partitions) → always run phase 2 (short limit, phase-1 incumbent as start) and drop points without it; (3) solution files are numbered by cable length, not by cost.

Decision 2026-10-19: no separate `optimizer_v2.py`. The submitted model is frozen as git tag `v1.0` (commit b7d7e0b); step B rewrites `optimizer.py` in place on `master`. Validation of the rewrite compares against the saved v1 runs in `results/` (testA_367: 10 cells, min-cost 8,285) and, if needed, against a checkout of `v1.0`.

### 16.1 B1 implemented (2026-10-19): placement x + partition count P + site y

`optimizer.py` / `optimizer_utils.py`, in place (v1 frozen as tag `v1.0`):
- Variables: `x[i,j]` (SC at site, latency-feasible pairs only), integer `P[j,a]` (partitions of ASIL a at site j, UB = min(max_per_asil_per_site, #SCs of that ASIL that may be hosted at j)), `y[j]` (site hosts a unit with >= 1 partition). The slot variables `y[j,a,p]`, `z[i,j,a,p]` and the slot-ordering constraint are gone; nothing is left to permute, so each design has one representation.
- Constraints: c1a unique site; c1b `x <= y`, `y <= Σ x`; c1c `P[j,a] <= Σ_{i of ASIL a} x[i,j]`; c1e `x[i,j] <= P[j,asil(i)]`; c1d per-site caps (`site.max_partitions`, `site.cpu/ram/rom_cap`, null = unlimited); c2 pooled capacity `Σ x·R·margin <= P·C_part·derating`. All other blocks (hardware, ports, shared buses, attachments, trunks, comm, per-flow routing, redundancy, contention, latency) unchanged, with `x_expr(i,j) = x[i,j]`.
- Pooled capacity is a relaxation of bin packing; after every solve `pack_partitions` (first-fit decreasing, then an exact packing model) assigns the SCs of each (site, ASIL) to concrete slots (this yields the `partitions` map `"<site>_asil<a>_p<k>"` the visualiser expects). An unpackable group adds the cut `P[j,a] >= (p+1)·(Σ_{i∈S} x[i,j] − |S| + 1)` and the solve repeats (`solve_info.packing_cuts`, cap `MAX_PACKING_CUTS`). With the current data (largest SC 7,000 DMIPS vs 100,000 per partition) it never triggers.
- Cost: `Σ cost_part·P[j,a] + Σ fixed_cost·y[j] + hardware + ports + cables`; `solution.site_cost` added. `configs/partitions.json` gained `partition.max_per_asil_per_site` (3, the v1 slot count) and a `site` block (all null / 0 = the v1 model); `config_reader.get_partitions` exposes them.
- Model size, 10 cells, `--uncertainty`: 9,375 variables / 8,475 constraints (v1: 10,765 / 9,616); 8,100 of them are still per-flow routing binaries → B2.
- Validation: job 453 (`results/b1val_453/`), same flags as job 367; expected min-cost 8,284.73, 3 sites (S30 52 SCs, S24 9, S01 4), 6 partitions.
- Validation result (job 453 vs 367, 10 cells, `--uncertainty`, 600 s): min-cost point identical, 8,284.7325 / 47.7531 m, both lexicographic phases OPTIMAL, same 3 sites, 6 partitions, DSP+HW_ACC at S30 (two ASIL-3 SCs swapped between S24 and S30 at equal cost and cable length). Runtime 215 s + 150 s vs 269 s + 244 s. Small instance (v2_lite, 4 cells) against a checkout of `v1.0`: both extremes identical (8,729.977 / 55.173 m and 9,747.447 / 47.707 m), 1,302 vs 1,738 variables. The min-cable extreme still hits the 600 s limit and skips phase 2 (cost 34.6k) → B3. Packing cuts: 0 in every solve.
- Cleanup (2026-10-19, user decision): the matplotlib solution figures are gone (`visualizer.py` keeps only the Pareto-front plot; `main.py` lost `--latex_topdown`, `--latex_architecture_pdf`, `--latex_compare_solutions`, `--topdown_dpi`, `--hide_peripheral_labels`, `--show_bus_utilization`; sbatch and README updated). Figures of a solution come only from `solution_paths.py` + `build_vehicle.py --solution` (results/vehicle_layout/solution_453/ for job 453). Still to decide: removing `data_generator` (random architectures, `--num_scs`, `--seed`), `--solver z3`, `--warm_start`, old `configs/full_architecture*` backups and the `ecu_zones`/`cable_channels` fallbacks.

### 16.2 B2 implemented (2026-10-19): candidate backbone paths, aggregated traffic

- Per-flow routing binaries `flow[t,u,v]` and their conservation / simple-path / per-flow switch constraints are gone. Backbone cables `comm[u,v,'ETH']` (integer) now exist for every site pair.
- `lnk[t,u,v] ∈ [0,1]` continuous, `lnk ≥ start_t(u) + end_t(v) − 1` where start/end are `x[src,u]`, `x[dst,v]` or the ETH attachment variables of a sensor/actuator; it only consumes link capacity, so the LP sets it to max(0, ·). Directed pair volume = Σ_t volume_t·lnk[t,u,v].
- Candidate paths per site pair (pairs with possible traffic): direct link + the (k−1) shortest routes over one relaying site, `k = configs/buses.json: backbone.candidate_paths` (3); lengths from the voxel distance table. `sel[u,v,p]` binary (≤ 1 path per pair, both directions), `f[u,v,p,d] ≥ 0` routed volume, `f ≤ vmax·sel` with vmax = volume of the flows that can use the pair in that direction, relay ⇒ `sel ≤ hw_use[w,SWITCH]`, link capacity per direction `Σ f ≤ cap_ETH·comm[a,b]`. Switch-only degree rule, link activation (`comm ≤ comm_ub·loc_active`) and port counting unchanged.
- `extract_solution` reports `backbone_routes` (chosen path and volumes per pair) and `comm_link_peak_load` from the routed volumes.
- Sizes: 4-cell lite instance 1,356 vars (v1 1,738); 33 cells 104,379 vars = 95,040 `lnk` (continuous) + 1,584 `sel` + 3,168 `f` + 2,145 `x` + …; ≈ 4.5 k binaries (v1 ≈ 100 k); 113 k constraints.
- Smoke (lite, 4 cells, v1.0 checkout as reference): both extremes identical (8,729.977 / 55.173 m; 9,747.447 / 47.707 m); the min-cable point uses a relayed route S01→S09→S25 (switch at S09).
- Validation runs: job 503 (10 cells, expect 8,284.73), job 504 (33 cells, first full instance, 3600 s per solve, `--write_lp`).
- Validation job 503 (B2 without the aggregated-conservation cut, 10 cells): min-cost point identical to v1/B1 (8,284.7325 / 47.7531 m, same sites/partitions/hardware, equal-cost SC split S24 17 / S30 44). But the LP relaxation is weaker (root 7,411 vs 7,561 in B1 / 7,581 in v1): phase 1 took 380 s / 16,393 nodes (B1: 215 s / 2,184). The min-cable extreme is now solved to optimality in phase 1 (41.158 m in 390 s; v1 could not within 600 s) and runs phase 2, which hits the limit (cost 9,980, gap 17 %) → B3. Fix added: C-NET-1b aggregated conservation (`Σ_v lnk[t,u,v] ≥ start_t(u) − end_t(u)` and the mirror at the end site), the per-site sum of v1's flow conservation; lite instance unchanged optima, root bound 8,101 → 8,135; 33 cells root 7,283 → 7,323. Runs: 529 (10 cells), 530 (33 cells) with the cut; 504 (33 cells) without it, for comparison.

### 16.3 B3 implemented (2026-10-19): phase-2 policy, numbering, LP-strengthening experiments

- Phase 2 of every lexicographic solve always runs, with the remaining time but at least `--phase2_time` (600 s); a phase-1-only point (arbitrary secondary objective, e.g. the 41 k-cost min-cable design of job 367) can no longer be returned.
- Pareto solutions are numbered by cost (`solution_1.json` = min cost). `solution.num_candidate_sites` records the candidate cells of the run; `solution_paths.py` copies it and `build_vehicle.py` prints it (no longer the count of `sites.json`).
- LP strengthening, both optional in `configs/buses.json: backbone` (default off): `conservation_cut` (aggregated flow conservation) and `path_link_cut` (a flow between two sites needs a selected path; a selected path has a cable on each link). Diagnosis of the 33-cell gap (bound stuck at 7,491 vs. incumbent 8,285): the relaxation opens every cell for free (`y` has no cost, partitions fractional), attaches every peripheral to its nearest cell and pays backbone cables only as load / capacity (≈ 7 % of a cable), so spreading SCs away from their sensors is almost free. Lite instance root bounds: none 8,101, conservation 8,135, both 8,212 (optima unchanged). 10-cell results: conservation alone (job 529) slower (LP heavier, phase 1 not finished in 600 s); jobs 533 (both cuts) and 534 (path cut only) pending.
- 10-cell results of the cut experiments (min-cost point, `--uncertainty`, 600 s, 64 threads; all reach the same optimum 8,284.7325 / 47.7531 m):

  | model | root bound | phase 1 | phase 2 |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | v1 (job 367) | 7,581 | 269 s, 1,437 nodes | 244 s |
  | B1 (453) | 7,561 | 215 s, 2,184 | 150 s |
  | B2, no cut (503) | 7,411 | 380 s, 16,393 | 187 s |
  | B2 + conservation (529) | 7,482 | time limit, gap 4.3 % | – |
  | B2 + both cuts (533) | 7,561 | 118 s, 1,487 | 139 s |
  | B2 + path-link cut (534) | 7,498 | 60 s, 2,081 | 55 s |

  Decision: `path_link_cut` on by default, `conservation_cut` off. The min-cable extreme is proven in phase 1 (41.158 m, 414 s with the path cut) but its phase 2 (min cost at fixed cable) does not finish in 600 s (gap 7 %, cost 9,980). 33 cells without cuts (job 504): phase 1 stops at 3,600 s with incumbent 8,285.25, bound 7,533, gap 9.1 %. Job 530 (conservation cut, 33 cells) cancelled: still at the root node after 30 min. Next: 33 cells with the path cut (job submitted 2026-10-19 evening).
- Lazy-traffic variant (2026-10-19 evening, `backbone.lazy_traffic`): no `lnk` / `f` variables; a MIPSOL callback checks every integer solution and adds lazy cuts "Σ_p sel[u,v,p] ≥ start_t(u) + end_t(v) − 1" (a pair with traffic needs a selected path) and "cap·comm[a,b] ≥ Σ vol_t·(start + end + sel − 2)" (link capacity) only where violated. Diagnosis behind it: with the 95 k `lnk` columns every B&B node of the 33-cell model costs 25–30 k simplex iterations (≈ 1 node per 50–100 s), so the search barely moves although the binaries are few. Results: lite instance identical optima with 456 instead of 1,356 variables; 10 cells identical optimum 8,284.7325 / 47.7531 in 11 s + 20 s (9,400 + 16,104 nodes) versus 115 s with the path cut and 513 s for v1. Job 544 = 33 cells with the lazy variant (1,800 s). Side result: job 539 (MIPFocus 3) found a 33-cell design at 8,254.76, below the 10-cell optimum, as expected from more candidate cells.
- 33-cell experiments with the full `lnk` model (1,800 s, min-cost only, 64 threads): branch priority on y/P/hw (job 538) 36 nodes, gap 9.3 %; `MIPFocus=3, Cuts=2` (539) 87 nodes, gap 8.3 %, incumbent 8,254.76 (< 10-cell optimum); both (540) 248 nodes, gap 9.6 %; path cut alone (536) 60 nodes after 2,000 s, gap 11 %. The LP per node (≈ 25–30 k simplex iterations) is the bottleneck, not the number of binaries. Lazy variant (546, 6,171 variables): ≈ 280 nodes/s, bound 7,590 after 270 s (above every 30-minute bound of the other variants).
- Job 504 (33 cells, full `lnk` model without cuts, old phase policy) finished: min-cost incumbent 8,285.25 after 3,600 s (gap 9.1 %; S29 54 SCs, S08 7, S03 4 — a cabin cell is used), min-cable extreme stopped at 41.11 m with gap 23.5 % and no phase 2 (cost 86.7 k, the junk-cost case the new phase-2 policy removes). Lazy run 546 at 1,130 s: 348 k nodes, incumbent 8,250.38, bound 7,779, gap 5.7 % and closing.
- Job 546 (33 cells, lazy, 1,800 s + 300 s): best 33-cell design so far, cost 8,250.38 (gap 4.5 %, 530 k nodes, 23.6 k lazy cuts): 2 ECUs, S03 (cabin, 57 SCs, DSP + HW_ACC) and S08 (trunk, 8 SCs, SWITCH), 6 partitions, one backbone link S03–S08; cable 51.5 m (phase 2 unfinished, gap 29 %). Rendered to results/vehicle_layout/solution_546/. New valid linking constraint c1f `Σ_a P[j,a] ≥ y[j]` (open site has a partition): lite optima unchanged; 10 cells root 7,362 → 7,392, cost phases 10 s + 5 s but the min-cable phase 1 slower (154 s vs 64 s); 33 cells root 7,262 → 7,313 (job 582 running).
- Job 582 (33 cells, lazy + c1f linking, 1,800 s): same incumbent 8,250.38, bound 8,131.7, gap 1.44 % (job 546 without c1f: 4.5 %), 404 k nodes. Decision: `lazy_traffic` on by default, c1f always on, `path_link_cut` on (static part only in lazy mode). Long run submitted to measure the time to a proven optimum on 33 cells (min-cost point, 6 h limit).
- **33 cells proven optimal** (job 634, lazy + c1f, 64 threads): min cost 8,250.378, proof in 7,302 s (2 h 2 min, 1.21 M nodes, 868 M simplex iterations). The 10-cell optimum was 8,284.73; the gap closed from 1.6 % (1.5 h) to 0 in the last 30 min once the incumbent 8,250.38 was found. Phase 2 (min cable at this cost, ≤ 1,800 s) running.

### 16.4 B2b implemented (2026-10-19 night): any cable type between control units

- Every cable type of `configs/buses.json` may connect two units (user decision: no per-type flag): `comm[u,v,ω]` per type, candidate paths per type (relay over a switch for Ethernet only, direct cable otherwise), `sel[u,v,ω,p]`. Each data flow may use every type whose capacity holds its volume (fallback: the largest type); `w[t,ω]` chooses the type when there is more than one option. Lazy cuts, cable upper bounds, port counting, interface activation and cable cost are per type. The explicit-traffic variant stays ETH-only (error otherwise). `solution.backbone_routes[*].iface`, `comm_link_peak_load` keys and the Blender cable colours follow the type.
- Regression with ETH only (results/cfg_ethonly): lite and 10-cell optima identical to before (8,284.7325 / 47.7531 m, 20 s + 5 s).
- All types, lite instance: min cost 8,352.46 instead of 8,729.98 (−4.3 %) using FlexRay, UART and SPI links for small flows; min-cable point 47.707 m at 9,585 instead of 9,747. With the current cost table (UART/SPI 5, LIN 10, CAN 20, FlexRay 30, ETH 100 per metre) the solver prefers the cheapest type that fits; whether UART/SPI belong in the cable table as inter-unit links is a data decision for the data pass (remove the type or set its parameters). Open: ASIL segregation on a shared inter-unit bus is not modelled (only for peripherals).

### 16.5 B4 implemented (2026-10-20): chain latency in milliseconds

- `tools/chains_from_graph.py` → `configs/chains.json`: 10 chains from the `receives_from` graph (per actuator the longest sensor path, 12–13 hops; per motion actuator the shortest path from a positioning sensor, 5–6 hops; HVAC unreachable). Each chain has `net_budget_ms` (time allowed on the network; brake/steering 20, motor 30, light 200, positioning chains half). Provisional values, to be replaced in the data pass.
- Message sizes from the existing volumes: `rate_hz` per sensor/actuator/SC (camera 30, lidar 10, IMU 100, GPS 10, Autoware 10, drivetrain 100), message kbit = volume (Mbit/s) × 1000 / rate_hz (camera frame 2,667 kbit, lidar scan 1,500 kbit). `buses.json`: `hop_latency_ms` per type (ETH 0.1, FlexRay 1, CAN 2, LIN 10, UART 1, SPI 0.1), capacity used as bit rate; `latency_per_meter` removed. `uncertainty.json: robust_latency` in ms (factor 1.1, sync 0.5, platform 2).
- Model (HANDOFF 14.3): a crossing of flow t between different sites over type ω costs hops × (kbit_t / rate_ω + hop_ω), with the robust factor and constants under `--robust_latency`; chain latency = sum over its crossings (sensor→driver and driver→actuator links of Ethernet peripherals included) ≤ budget. Enforced as lazy cuts next to the traffic cuts; reported per chain (`solution.chain_latency`). The old per-metre latency constraints, the peripheral max-latency pre-elimination and `comm_matrix.max_latency` are gone.
- Tests (10 cells, all cable types, lazy, min-cost point): default budgets → 7,961.27 unchanged, chains far from their budgets (max 74 / 200 ms); budgets × 0.25 → 7,987.83 (+0.33 %), hub moves S30 → S01, SPI links replaced by CAN, the brake chain loses its crossing, both light chains bind at 47.1 / 50 ms. Proof 238–293 s phase 1, 160–180 s phase 2.

### 15.4 Visualisation conventions borrowed from industry (PREEvision, 2026-10-20)

Vector, "Design and Optimization of E/E Architectures: PREEvision as Model-Based Tool for the Next Generation", technical article, AutomobilElektronik 2016 (vendor material, cited for conventions only, not as a scientific source). Views of the tool and what we take over:
- Abstraction layers (Fig. 4): Requirements → Logical Architecture → System-Software Architecture → Hardware Network Topology → Electric Circuit → Wiring Harness → Geometrical Topology, with Communication alongside. Our result presentation follows the same stack: software mapping view (partitions, SWCs), hardware network topology view, geometrical topology (Blender renders), plus the chain/latency table as the communication aspect.
- Hardware network topology diagram (ECU boxes in a grid, buses as orthogonal colour-coded lines, multi-drop buses as one line with stubs, point-to-point Ethernet links, gateways/switches as nodes, sensors as small boxes hanging on their bus): to be added as a second view of `tools/architecture_diagram.py` (today the backbone is drawn as arcs above the unit boxes).
- Multidimensional evaluation (Fig. 5): radar chart over cost, weight, installation space, bus load, … for several designs — planned for step D to compare Pareto points and archetype templates (cost, cable length/mass, units, partitions, bus load, latency margin, tolerated growth α).
- Wiring harness view (Fig. 3): segments with lengths, splices, connectors — the Blender route view already shows the harness; a 2D schematic with segment lengths is optional later.

### 16.6 Step C: decisions (2026-10-20, user) and model sketch

- Critical set: SCs with ASIL ≥ C (47 of 65). Failure set: every active cell, every switch, every backbone cable (all three classes).
- Standby type is a decision of the optimiser per critical SC: hot standby (second instance runs, consumes resources, generates the same traffic, instant take-over) or cold standby (capacity reserved at the standby cell, no traffic, take-over after the recovery time). Cold is admissible only when the fault-tolerant time of every chain the SC belongs to is at least the platform recovery time (Becker et al. 2014, section 14.4); chains therefore get `ftti_ms`, the platform a `recovery_ms`. Primary and standby on different cells; for cable/switch failures the standby's paths must not share the failed element with the primary's (disjoint routes, Streichert 2007). Checked per failure scenario in the lazy callback; violated scenarios add cuts.
- Growth: demand_i(α) = demand_i · (1 + α · g[domain_i]); α is the global growth level (Pareto axis), g[d] the relative growth of a domain. Proposed profiles (parameters, to be swept): ADAS/Driver 1.0, Connectivity 0.5, Infotainment 0.5, DriveTrain 0.3, BodyComfort 0.2. Capacity constraints hold at the chosen α; the Pareto sweep solves min cost for α ∈ {0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.5, 2}, once with and once without the failure constraints (D5).
- Outputs: cost–α curves, design per α (architecture diagram marks standby instances), the scenario that forced each standby, chain latencies in the nominal case (degraded bounds later).

### 16.7 C1 implemented (2026-10-20): software growth

- `configs/uncertainty.json: growth` — profiles g per domain (ADAS, Driver 1.0; Connectivity, Infotainment 0.7; DriveTrain, BodyComfort 0.4; derived from the 10–20 %/year range of Ebert and Jones 2009, section 14.4), `alpha_levels` [0, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3] (α = 1: reference domain doubled ≈ 4–7 years at those rates; α = 3 ≈ 8–15 years). Sixth uncertainty block `growth` (`--growth`, included in `--uncertainty`); no other switches, all values in the JSON.
- Model: the demand multiplier of every SC becomes margin × (1 + α · g[domain]) in the capacity constraints and in the partition packing check; nothing else changes. `optimize_growth_sweep` solves min cost (phase 2 cable) per level and stops at the first infeasible level; `solve_info.growth_alpha`, `run_info.solutions[*].growth_alpha`; figure `cost_vs_growth.pdf` (+ .dat) replaces the cost–cable Pareto plot in growth runs; the architecture diagram title shows the level.
- Provisional cell capacity (`configs/partitions.json: site`): at most 4 partitions and 200,000 / 64,000 / 128,000 DMIPS/MB/MB per cell (two partitions' worth); without a cell cap, growth would only add partitions to the same cell and never split a unit.
